. To avoid this, we can just keep a factorization
U_{B}
and
V_{B}
. We are still able to run algorithm
L1LowRankApproxB
. Because
s=\poly(k)
, the running time of algorithm
L1LowRankApproxB
is still
O(\nnz(A))+(n+d)\poly(k)
.

By the definition of
B
, we have that
B
has rank at most
s
. Suppose we then solve
\ell_{1}
-low rank approximation problem for rank-
s
matrix
B
, finding a rank-
k

g
-approximation matrix
UV
. Due to Lemma
C.15
, we have that if
B
is an
f
-approximation solution to
A
, then
UV
is also an
O(fg)
-approximation solution to
A
,

\|UV-A\|_{1}\leq O(\log d)\cdot\poly(k)\cdot g\OPT.

Using Theorem
C.19
we have that
g=\poly(s)
, which completes the proof.

∎

C.4
\widetilde{O}(k)
-approximation for an arbitrary matrix
A

Algorithm 4

\widetilde{O}(k)
-approximation Algorithm

1:

procedure

L1LowRankApproxK
(
A,n,d,k
)
\triangleright
Theorem
C.7

2:

r\leftarrow O(k\log k),m\leftarrow t_{1}\leftarrow O(r\log r),t_{2}\leftarrow O(m\log m)
.

3:

Guess a diagonal matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with only
r

1
s.
\triangleright

R
selects
r
columns of
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
.

4:

Compute a sampling and rescaling matrix
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},T_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
corresponding to the Lewis weights of
AR
, and let them have
m,t_{1}
nonzero entries on the diagonals, respectively.

5:

Compute a sampling and rescaling matrix
T_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
according to the Lewis weights of
(DA)^{\top}
, and let it have
t_{2}
nonzero entries on the diagonal.

6:

Solve
\min_{X,Y}\|T_{1}ARXYDAT_{2}-T_{1}AT_{2}\|_{1}
.

7:

Take the best solution
X,Y
over all guesses of
R
.

8:

return

ARX
,
YDA
.

9:

end

procedure

Theorem C.7
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, there exists an algorithm that takes
\poly(n)\cdot d^{\widetilde{O}(k)}\cdot 2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}
time and outputs two matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
such that

\displaystyle\|UV-A\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k)\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

We define

\displaystyle\OPT:=\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}.

Let
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
satisfy

\displaystyle\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}=\OPT.

Let
S^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
denote the sampling and rescaling matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
(V^{*})^{\top}
, where the number of nonzero entries on the diagonal of
S
is
s=r=O(k\log k)
. Let
R^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
denote a diagonal matrix such that
\forall i\in[d]
, if
S_{i,i}\not=0
, then
R_{i,i}=1
, and if
S_{i,i}=0
, then
R_{i,i}=0
.
Since
\mathrm{rowspan}(R^{\top}A^{\top})=\mathrm{rowspan}(S^{\top}A^{\top})
,

\displaystyle\min_{Z\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|ASZV-A\|_{1}=\min_{Z\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|ARZV-A\|_{1}.

Combining with Part III of Corollary
C.2
, there exists a
\rank
-
k
solution in the column span of
AR
, which means,

\displaystyle\min_{Z\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|ARZV-A\|_{1}\leq O(\sqrt{r})\OPT.

(10)

Because the number of
1
s of
R
is
r
, and the size of the matrix is
d\times d
, there are
{d\choose r}=d^{\widetilde{O}(k)}
different choices for locations of
1
s on the diagonal of
R
. We cannot compute
R
directly, but we can guess all the choices of locations of
1
s. Regarding
R
as selecting
r
columns of
A
, then there are
d^{\widetilde{O}(k)}
choices. There must exist a “correct” way of selecting a subset of columns over all all choices. After trying all of them, we will have chosen the right one.

For a fixed guess
R
, we can compute
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, which is a sampling and rescaling matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
AR
, and let
m=O(k\log^{2}k)
be the number of nonzero entries on the diagonal of
D
.

By Equation (
10
), there exists a
W\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}
such that,

\displaystyle\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|ARWV-A\|_{1}\leq O(\sqrt{r})\OPT.

(11)

We define
\widehat{V}_{i}=\underset{V_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\arg\min}\|DARWV_{i}-DA_{i}\|_{2}
,
\forall i\in[d]
, which means
\widehat{V}_{i}=(DARW)^{\dagger}DA_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then
\widehat{V}=(DARW)^{\dagger}DA\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
. We define
V^{*}=\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\arg\min}\|ARWV-A\|_{1}
. Then, by Claim
B.10
, it has

\displaystyle\|DARW\widehat{V}-DA\|_{1}\leq O(\sqrt{m})\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|DARWV-DA\|_{1}.

By applying Lemma
D.11
, Lemma
D.8
and Equation (
11
), we can show

\displaystyle\|ARW\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}\leq O(\sqrt{m})\|ARWV^{*}-A\|_{1}\leq O(\sqrt{mr})\OPT\leq\widetilde{O}(k)\OPT.

Plugging
\widehat{V}=(DARW)^{\dagger}DA
into
\|ARW\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|ARW(DARW)^{\dagger}DA-A\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k)\OPT.

and it is clear that,

\displaystyle\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times m}}\|ARXYDA-A\|_{1}\leq\|ARW(DARW)^{\dagger}DA-A\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k)\OPT.

Recall that we guessed
R
, so it is known. We can compute
T_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, which is a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
AR
, and
t_{1}=O(r\log r)
is the number of nonzero entries on the diagonal of
T_{1}
.

Also,
DA
is known, and the number of nonzero entries in
D
is
m=O(k\log^{2}k)
. We can compute
T_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, which is a sampling and rescaling matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
(DA)^{\top}
, and
t_{2}=O(m\log m)
is the number of nonzero entries on the diagonal of
T_{2}
.

Define
X^{*},Y^{*}=\underset{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times m}}{\arg\min}\|T_{1}(AR)XY(DA)T_{2}-T_{1}AT_{2}\|_{1}
.
Thus, using Lemma
D.10
, we have

\displaystyle\|(AR)X^{*}Y^{*}(DA)-A\|_{1}\leq\widetilde{O}(k)\OPT.

To find
X^{*},Y^{*}
, we need to solve this minimization problem

\displaystyle\underset{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times m}}{\min}\|T_{1}(AR)XY(DA)T_{2}-T_{1}AT_{2}\|_{1},

which can be solved by a polynomial system verifier (see more discussion in Section
B.1
and
B.5
).

In the next paragraphs, we explain how to solve the above problem by using a polynomial system verifier. Notice that
T_{1}(AR)
is known and
(DA)T_{2}
is also known. First, we create
r\times k
variables for the matrix
X
, i.e., one variable for each entry of
X
. Second, we create
k\times m
variables for matrix
Y
, i.e., one variable for each entry of
Y
. Putting it all together and creating
t_{1}\times t_{2}
variables
\sigma_{i,j},\forall i\in[t_{1}],j\in[t_{2}]
for handling the unknown signs, we write down the following optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{X,Y}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{t_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{t_{2}}\sigma_{i,j}(T_{1}(AR)XY(DA)T_{2})_{i,j}

s.t

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j}^{2}=1,\forall i\in[t_{1}],j\in[t_{2}]

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j}\cdot(T_{1}(AR)XY(DA)T_{2})_{i,j}\geq 0,\forall i\in[t_{1}],j\in[t_{2}].

Notice that the number of constraints is
O(t_{1}t_{2})=\widetilde{O}(k^{2})
, the maximum degree is
O(1)
, and the number of variables
O(t_{1}t_{2}+km+rk)=\widetilde{O}(k^{2})
. Thus the running time is,

\displaystyle(\#\constraints\cdot\#\degree)^{O(\#\variables)}=2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}.

To use a polynomial system verifier, we need to discuss the bit complexity. Suppose that all entries are multiples of
\delta
, and the maximum is
\Delta
, i.e., each entry
\in\{-\Delta,\cdots,-2\delta,-\delta,0,\delta,2\delta,\cdots,\Delta\}
, and
\Delta/\delta=2^{\poly(nd)}
. Then the running time is
O(\poly(\Delta/\delta))\cdot 2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}=\poly(nd)2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}
.

Also, a polynomial system verifier is able to tell us whether there exists a solution in a semi-algebraic set. In order to find the solution, we need to do a binary search over the cost
C
. In each step of the binary search we use a polynomial system verifier to determine if there exists a solution in,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{t_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{t_{2}}\sigma_{i,j}(T_{1}(AR)XY(DA)T_{2})_{i,j}\leq C

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j}^{2}=1,\forall i\in[t_{1}],j\in[t_{2}]

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sigma_{i,j}\cdot(T_{1}(AR)XY(DA)T_{2})_{i,j}\geq 0,\forall i\in[t_{1}],j\in[t_{2}].

In order to do binary search over the cost, we need to know an upper bound on the cost and also a lower bound on the minimum nonzero cost. The upper bound on the cost is
C_{\max}=O(nd\Delta)
, and the minimum nonzero cost is
C_{\min}=2^{-\Omega(\poly(nd))}
. Thus, the total number of steps for binary search is
O(\log(C_{\max}/C_{\min}))
. Overall, the running time is

\displaystyle nd^{\widetilde{O}(k)}\cdot 2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}\cdot\log(\Delta/\delta)\cdot\log(C_{\max}/C_{\min})=\poly(n)d^{\widetilde{O}(k)}2^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Instead of solving an
\ell_{1}
problem at the last step of
L1LowRankApproxK
by using a polynomial system verifier, we can just solve a Frobenius norm minimization problem. This slightly improves the running time and pays an extra
\poly(k)
factor in the approximation ratio. Thus, we obtain the following corollary,

Corollary C.8
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, there exists an algorithm that takes
\poly(n)\cdot d^{\widetilde{O}(k)}
time which outputs two matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
such that

\displaystyle|UV-A\|_{1}\leq\poly(k)\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability
9/10
.

C.5
Rank-
2k
and
O(1)
-approximation algorithm for an arbitrary matrix
A

In this section, we show how to output a rank-
2k
solution that is able to achieve an
O(1)
-approximation.

Algorithm 5

Bicriteria
O(1)
-approximation Algorithm

1:

procedure

L1LowRankApproxBicriteria
(
A,n,d,k
)
\triangleright
Theorem
C.9

2:

U_{B},V_{B}\leftarrow\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|UV-A\|_{F}
.

3:

r\leftarrow O(k\log k)
.

4:

Guess a diagonal matrix
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
r
nonzero entries.

5:

Guess matrix
DU\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}
.

6:

Find
V_{A}
by solving
\min_{V}\|DUV-D(A-B)\|_{1}
.

7:

Find
U_{A}
by solving
\min_{U}\|UV_{A}-(A-B)\|_{1}
.

8:

Take the best solution
\begin{bmatrix}U_{A}&U_{B}\end{bmatrix}
,
\begin{bmatrix}V_{A}\\
V_{B}\end{bmatrix}
over all guesses.

9:

return

\begin{bmatrix}U_{A}&U_{B}\end{bmatrix}
,
\begin{bmatrix}V_{A}\\
V_{B}\end{bmatrix}
.

10:

end

procedure

Theorem C.9
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists an algorithm which takes
(nd)^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}
time to output two matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2k}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{2k\times d}
,

\displaystyle\|UV-A\|_{1}\lesssim\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1},

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

We define
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be the optimal solution, i.e.,

\displaystyle U^{*}V^{*}=\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\arg\min}\|UV-A\|_{1},

and define
\OPT=\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}
.

Solving the Frobenius norm problem, we can find a factorization of a rank-
k
matrix
B=U_{B}V_{B}
where
U_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, and
B
satisfies

\|A-B\|_{1}\leq\alpha_{B}\OPT
for an
\alpha_{B}=\sqrt{nd}
.

Let
D
be a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
U^{*}
, and let the number of nonzero entries on the diagonal be
t=O(k\log k)
.

By Lemma
D.11
and Lemma
D.8
, the solution of
\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|DU^{*}V-D(A-B)\|_{1}
together with
U^{*}
gives an
O(1)
-approximation to
A-B
. In order to compute
D
we need to know
U^{*}
. Although we do not know
U^{*}
, there still exists a way to figure out the Lewis weights. The idea is the same as in the previous discussion Lemma
C.10
“Guessing Lewis weights”.
By Claim
C.11
and Claim
C.12
, the total number of possible
D
is
n^{O(t)}
.

Lemma
C.10
tries to find a
\rank
-
k
solution when all the entries in
A
are integers at most
\poly(nd)
. Here we focus on a bicriteria algorithm that outputs a
\rank
-
2k
matrix without such a strong bit complexity assumption.
We can show a better claim
C.13
, which is that the total number of possible
DU^{*}
is
N^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}
where
N=\poly(n)
is the
number of choices for a single entry in
U^{*}
.

We explain how to obtain an upper bound on
N
. Consider the optimum
\|U^{*}V^{*}-(A-B)\|_{1}
. We can always change the basis so assume
V^{*}
is an Auerbach basis (i.e., an
\ell_{1}
-well-conditioned basis discussed in Section
1
), so
e\|x\|_{1}\geq\|xV^{*}\|_{1}\geq\|x\|_{1}/f
, where
e,f=\poly(k)
. Then no entry of
U^{*}
is larger than
2f\|A-B\|_{1}
, otherwise we could replace
U^{*}
with
0
and get a better solution. Also any entry smaller than
\|A-B\|_{1}/(enk\alpha_{B}100)
can be replaced with
0
as this will incur additive error at most
\OPT/100
. So if we round to integer multiples of
\|A-B\|_{1}/(enk\alpha_{B}100)
we only have
O(enk\alpha_{B}f)
possibilities for each entry of
U^{*}
and still have an
O(1)
-approximation. We will just refer to this rounded
U^{*}
as
U^{*}
, abusing notation.

Let
{\cal U}
denote the set of all the matrices
U
that we guess. From the above discussion, we conclude that, there exists a
U\in{\cal U}
such that
\|UV^{*}-(A-B)\|_{1}\leq O(\OPT)
.

For each guess of
DU^{*}
and
D
, we find
V_{A},U_{A}
in the following way.
We find
V_{A}
by using a linear program to solve,

\displaystyle\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|DU^{*}V-D(A-B)\|_{1}.

Given
V_{A}
and
A
, we write down a linear program to solve this problem,

\displaystyle\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|UV_{A}-(A-B)\|_{1},

which takes
\poly(ndk)
time. Then we obtain
U_{A}
.

Recall that
V_{B},U_{B}
are the two factors of
B
and it is a
\rank
-
k
,
\alpha_{B}
-approximation solution to
\underset{U,V}{\min}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\biggl\|\begin{bmatrix}U_{A}&U_{B}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}V_{A}\\
V_{B}\end{bmatrix}-A\biggr\|_{1}=\biggl\|U_{A}V_{A}-(A-U_{B}V_{B})\biggr\|_{1}=\biggl\|U_{A}V_{A}-(A-B)\biggr\|_{1}.

Because there must exist a pair
U_{A},V_{A}
satisfying
\|U_{A}V_{A}-(A-B)\|_{1}\leq O(\OPT)
, it follows that by taking the best solution
\begin{bmatrix}U_{A}&U_{B}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}V_{A}\\
V_{B}\end{bmatrix}
over all guesses, we obtain an
O(1)
-approximation solution.

Overall, the running time is
(nd)^{\widetilde{O}(k^{2})}
.
∎

Lemma C.10
.

Given an
n\times d
matrix
A
with integers bounded by
\poly(n)
, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists an algorithm which takes
(nd)^{\widetilde{O}(k^{3})}
time to output two matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, such that

\displaystyle\|UV-A\|_{1}\lesssim\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1},

Proof.

We define
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be the optimal solution, i.e.,

\displaystyle U^{*},V^{*}=\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\arg\min}\|UV-A\|_{1},

and define
\OPT=\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}
.

Let
D
denote a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
U^{*}
, and let the number of nonzero entries on the diagonal be
t=O(k\log k)
.

By Lemma
D.11
and Lemma
D.8
, the solution to
\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|DU^{*}V-DA\|_{1}
together with
U^{*}
gives an
O(1)
-approximation to
A
. In order to compute
D
, we need to know
U^{*}
. Although we do not know
U^{*}
, there still exists a way to figure out the Lewis weights. We call the idea “Guessing Lewis weights”. We will explain this idea in the next few paragraphs.

First, we can guess the nonzero entries on the diagonal, because the number of choices is small.

Claim C.11
.

The number of possible choice of
\supp(D)
is at most
n^{O(t)}
.

Proof.

The matrix has dimension
n\times n
and the number of nonzero entries is
t
. Thus the number of possible choices is at most
\sum_{i=1}^{t}{n\choose t}=n^{O(t)}
.
∎

Second, we can guess the value of each probability. For each probability, it is trivially at most
1
. If the probability is less than
1/(\poly(n)k\log k)
, then we will never sample that row with high probability. It means that we can truncate the probability if it is below that threshold. We can also round each probability to
2^{-i}
which only loses another constant factor in the approximation ratio. Thus, we have:

Claim C.12
.

The total number of possible
D
is
n^{O(t)}=n^{\widetilde{O}(k)}
.

Since
\Delta/\delta\leq\poly(n)
and the entries of
A
are in
\{-\Delta,-\Delta+\delta,\ldots,-2\delta,-\delta,0,\delta,2\delta,\cdots,\Delta-\delta,\Delta\}
, we can lower bound the cost of
\|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}
given that it is non-zero by
(nd\Delta/\delta)^{-O(k)}
(if it is zero then
A
has rank at most
k
and we output
A
) using Lemma 4.1 in
[
CW09
]
and relating entrywise
\ell_{1}
-norm to Frobenius norm. We can assume
V
is an
\ell_{1}
well-conditioned basis, since we can replace
U^{*}
with
U^{*}R^{-1}
and
V
with
RV
for any invertible linear transformation
R
. By properties of such basis, we can discretize the entries of
U^{*}
to integer multiples of
(nd\Delta/\delta)^{-O(k)}
while preserving relative error. Hence we can correctly guess each entry of
DU^{*}
in
\left(n^{O(k)}\right)
time.

Claim C.13
.

The total number of possible
DU^{*}
is
n^{\widetilde{O}(k^{3})}
.

In the following, let
DU
denote a guess of
DU^{*}
.
Now the problem remaining is to solve
\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|DUV-DA\|_{1}
. Since we already know
DA
can be computed, and we know
DU
, we can solve this multiple regression problem by running linear programming. Thus the running time of this step is in
\poly(nd)
. After we get such a solution
V
, we use a linear program to solve
\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|DUV-DA\|_{1}
. Then we can get
U
.

After we guess all the choices of
D
and
DU^{*}
, we must find a solution
U,V
which gives an
O(1)
approximation. The total running time is
n^{\widetilde{O}(k)}\cdot n^{\widetilde{O}(k^{3})}\cdot\poly(nd)=(nd)^{\widetilde{O}(k^{3})}
.
∎

C.6
CUR decomposition for an arbitrary matrix
A

Algorithm 6

CUR Decomposition Algorithm

1:

procedure

L1LowRankApproxCUR
(
A,n,d,k
)
\triangleright
Theorem
C.14

2:

U_{B},V_{B}\leftarrow
L1LowRankApproxPolykLogd
(
A,n,d,k
).

3:

Let
D_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be the sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
B_{1}=U_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, and let
D_{1}
have
d_{1}=O(k\log k)
nonzero entries.

4:

Let
D_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be the sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
B_{2}^{\top}=\left((D_{1}B_{1})^{\dagger}D_{1}A\right)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
, and let
D_{2}
have
d_{2}=O(k\log k)
nonzero entries.

5:

C\leftarrow AD_{2}
,
U\leftarrow(B_{2}D_{2})^{\dagger}(D_{1}B_{1})^{\dagger}
, and
R\leftarrow D_{1}A
.

6:

return

C,U,R
.

7:

end

procedure

Theorem C.14
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, for any
k\geq 1
, there exists an algorithm which takes
O(\nnz(A))+(n+d)\poly(k)
time to output three matrices
C\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times c}
with columns from
A
,
U\in\mathbb{R}^{c\times r}
, and
R\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
with rows from
A
, such that
\rank(CUR)=k
,
c=O(k\log k)
,
r=O(k\log k)
, and

\displaystyle\|CUR-A\|_{1}\leq\poly(k)\log d\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1},

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

We define

\displaystyle\OPT:=\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A_{k}-A\|_{1}.

Due to Theorem
C.6
, we can output two matrices
U_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,
V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
such that
U_{B}V_{B}
gives a
\rank
-
k
, and
\poly(k)\log d
-approximation solution to
A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|U_{B}V_{B}-A\|_{1}\leq\poly(k)\log d\OPT.

(12)

By Section
B.3
, we can compute
D_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
which is a sampling and rescaling matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
B_{1}=U_{B}
in
O(n\poly(k))
time, and there are
d_{1}=O(k\log k)
nonzero entries on the diagonal of
D_{1}
.

Define
V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be the optimal solution of
\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|B_{1}V-A\|_{1}
,
\widehat{V}=(D_{1}B_{1})^{\dagger}D_{1}A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,
U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
to be the optimal solution of
\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}
, and
V^{\prime}
to be the optimal solution of
\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|D_{1}A-D_{1}B_{1}V\|_{1}
.

By Claim
B.10
, we have

\displaystyle\|D_{1}B_{1}\widehat{V}-D_{1}A\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{d_{1}}\|D_{1}B_{1}V^{\prime}-D_{1}A\|_{1}.

Due to Lemma
D.11
and Lemma
D.8
, with constant probability, we have

\displaystyle\|B_{1}\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{d_{1}}\alpha_{D_{1}}\|B_{1}V^{*}-A\|_{1},

where
\alpha_{D_{1}}=O(1)
.

Now, we can show,

\displaystyle\|U_{1}\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \|B_{1}\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }U_{1}=\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\arg\min}\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\ \sqrt{d_{1}}\|B_{1}V^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \sqrt{d_{1}}\|U_{B}V_{B}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \poly(k)\log d\OPT.

by Equation (
12
)

(13)

We define
B_{2}=\widehat{V}
, then we replace
\widehat{V}
by
B_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
and look at this objective function,

\displaystyle\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|UB_{2}-A\|_{1},

where
U^{*}
denotes the optimal solution. We use a sketching matrix to sketch the
\mathrm{RHS}
of matrix
UB_{2}-A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
D_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
denote a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix corresponding to the Lewis weights of
B_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
, and let the number of nonzero entries on the diagonal of
D_{2}
be
d_{2}=O(k\log k)
.
We define
\widehat{U}=AD_{2}(B_{2}D_{2})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
,

U^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
to be the optimal solution of
\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|(UB_{2}-A)D_{2}\|_{1}
. Recall that
U_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
is the optimal of
\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|UB_{2}-A\|_{1}
.

By Claim
B.10
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}B_{2}D_{2}-AD_{2}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{d_{2}}\|U^{\prime}B_{2}D_{2}-AD_{2}\|_{1}.

According to Lemma
D.8
and Lemma
D.11
, with constant probability,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{U}B_{2}-A\|_{1}\leq\alpha_{D_{2}}\sqrt{d_{2}}\|U_{1}B_{2}-A\|_{1},

where
\alpha_{B_{2}}=O(1)
.

We have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \quad\penalty\ \|\widehat{U}B_{2}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \sqrt{d_{2}}\alpha_{D_{2}}\|U_{1}B_{2}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \sqrt{d_{2}}\alpha_{D_{2}}\|U_{1}\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }B_{2}=\widehat{V}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \poly(k)\log(d)\OPT.

by Equation (
C.6
)

Notice that
\widehat{U}B_{2}=AD_{2}(B_{2}D_{2})^{\dagger}(D_{1}B_{1})^{\dagger}D_{1}A
.
Setting

\displaystyle C=AD_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{2}},U=(B_{2}D_{2})^{\dagger}(D_{1}B_{1})^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{1}},\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }R=D_{1}A\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d},

we get the desired CUR decomposition,

\displaystyle\|\underbrace{AD_{2}}_{C}\cdot\underbrace{(B_{2}D_{2})^{\dagger}(D_{1}B_{1})^{\dagger}}_{U}\cdot\underbrace{D_{1}A}_{R}-A\|_{1}\leq\poly(k)\log(d)\OPT.

with
\rank(CUR)=k
. Overall, the running time is
O(\nnz(A))+(n+d)\poly(k)
.

∎

C.7
Rank-
r
matrix
B

C.7.1
Properties

Lemma C.15
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
\OPT=\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ A_{k}}{\min}\|A-A_{k}\|_{1}
. For any
r\geq k
, if rank-
r
matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is an
f
-approximation to
A
, i.e.,

\|B-A\|_{1}\leq f\cdot\OPT,

and
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
is a
g
-approximation to
B
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|UV-B\|_{1}\leq g\cdot\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ B_{k}}{\min}\|B_{k}-B\|_{1},

then,

\|UV-A\|_{1}\lesssim gf\cdot\OPT.

Proof.

We define
\widetilde{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},\widetilde{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be two matrices, such that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U}\widetilde{V}-B\|_{1}\leq g\min_{\rank-k\penalty\ B_{k}}\|B_{k}-B\|_{1},

and also define,

\displaystyle\widehat{U},\widehat{V}=\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\arg\min}\|UV-B\|_{1}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }U^{*},V^{*}=\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\arg\min}\|UV-A\|_{1},

Then,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U}\widetilde{V}-A\|_{1}\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U}\widetilde{V}-B\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle g\|\widehat{U}\widehat{V}-B\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

by definition

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle g\|U^{*}V^{*}-B\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\|\widehat{U}\widehat{V}-B\|_{1}\leq\|U^{*}V^{*}-B\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle g\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}+g\|B-A\|_{1}+\|B-A\|_{1}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle g\OPT+(g+1)\|B-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ definition\penalty\ of\penalty\ }\OPT

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle g\OPT+(g+1)f\cdot\OPT

by
B
is
f
-approximation to
A

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle gf\OPT.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.16
.

Given a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
with rank
r
, for any
1\leq k<r
, for any fixed
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, choose a Cauchy matrix
S
with
m=O(r\log r)
rows and rescaled by
\Theta(1/m)
. With probability
.999
for all
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, we have

\displaystyle\|SU^{*}V-SB\|_{1}\geq\|U^{*}V-B\|_{1}.

Proof.

This follows by definitions in Section
D
and Lemma
D.23
.
∎

Lemma C.17
.

Given a matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
with rank
r
, for any
1\leq k<r
, for any fixed
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, choose a Cauchy matrix
S
with
m
rows and rescaled by
\Theta(1/m)
. We have

\displaystyle\|SU^{*}V^{*}-SB\|_{1}\leq O(r\log r)\|U^{*}V^{*}-B\|_{1},

with probability
.999
.

Proof.

Let
U
denote a well-conditioned basis of
[U^{*}V^{*},B]
, then
U
has
\widetilde{d}=O(r)
columns. We have

\displaystyle\|SU\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|(SU_{i})_{j}|

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{l=1}^{n}S_{j,l}U_{l,i}|

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }S_{j,l}\sim C(0,1)

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|c_{i,j}|

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }c_{i,j}\sim C(0,\|U_{i}\|_{1})

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\widetilde{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|U_{i}\|_{1}\cdot w_{i+(j-1)d},

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }w_{i,j}\sim|C(0,1)|

where the last step follows since each
w_{i}
can be thought of as a clipped half-Cauchy random variable. Define
d^{\prime}=m\widetilde{d}
. Define
event
\xi_{i}
to be the situation when
w_{i}<D
(we will choose
D
later), and define event

\xi=\xi_{1}\cap\xi_{2}\cap\cdots\cap\xi_{d^{\prime}}
. Using a similar proof as Lemma
D.12
,
which is also similar to previous work
[
Ind06
,
SW11
,
CDMI+13
]
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\Pr\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|SU_{i}\|_{1}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|U_{i}\|_{1}t\biggr]\lesssim\frac{\log d^{\prime}}{t}+\frac{d^{\prime}}{D}.

Choosing
t=\Theta(\log d^{\prime})
and
D=\Theta(d^{\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|SU_{i}\|_{1}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|U_{i}\|_{1}O(\log d^{\prime})\biggr]\leq\frac{1}{C},

for a constant
C
.
Condition on the above event.
Let
y=Ux
, for some
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then for any
y
,

\displaystyle\|Sy\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \|SUx\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \sum_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}\|SU_{j}x_{j}\|_{1}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \sum_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}|x_{j}|\cdot\|SU_{j}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x\|_{\infty}\log(d^{\prime})\sum_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}\|U_{j}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim r\log r\|y\|_{1},

where the last step follows by
\sum_{j=1}^{d^{\prime}}\|U_{j}\|_{1}\leq d^{\prime}
and
\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\|Ux\|_{1}=\|y\|_{1}
. Choosing
C=1000
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.18
.

Given a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
with rank
O(r)
, choose a random matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
with each entry drawn from
a standard Cauchy distribution and scaled by
\Theta(1/m)
. We have that

\displaystyle\|SM\|_{1}\leq O(r\log r)\|M\|_{1},

holds with probability
.999
.

Proof.

Let
U\in\mathbb{R}^{O(r)}
be the well-conditioned basis of
M
. Then each column of
M
can be expressed by
Ux
for some
x
.
We then follow the same proof as that of Lemma
C.17
.
∎

C.7.2
\poly(k,r)
-approximation for rank-
r
matrix
B

Algorithm 7

\poly(r,k)
-approximation Algorithm for Rank-
r
matrix
B

1:

procedure

L1LowRankApproxB
(
U_{B},V_{B},n,d,k,r
)
\triangleright
Theorem
C.19

2:

Set
s\leftarrow\widetilde{O}(r),r^{\prime}\leftarrow\widetilde{O}(r)
,
t_{1}\leftarrow\widetilde{O}(r)
,
t_{2}\leftarrow\widetilde{O}(r)
.

3:

Choose dense Cauchy matrices
S\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times n}
,
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r^{\prime}}
,
T_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{1}\times n}
,
T_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times t_{2}}
.

4:

Compute
S\cdot U_{B}\cdot V_{B}
,
U_{B}\cdot V_{B}\cdot R
and
T_{1}\cdot U_{B}\cdot V_{B}\cdot T_{2}
.

5:

Compute
XY=\arg\min_{X,Y}\|T_{1}U_{B}V_{B}RXYSU_{B}V_{B}T_{2}-T_{1}U_{B}V_{B}T_{2}\|_{F}
.

6:

return

U_{B}V_{B}RX,YSU_{B}V_{B}
.

7:

end

procedure

Theorem C.19
.

Given a factorization of a
\rank
-
r
matrix
B=U_{B}V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, where
U_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r},V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
, for any
1\leq k\leq r
there exists an algorithm which takes
(n+d)\cdot\poly(k)
time to output two matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
such that

\|UV-B\|_{1}\leq\poly(r)\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ B_{k}}{\min}\|B_{k}-B\|_{1},

holds with probability
9/10
.

Proof.

We define

\displaystyle\OPT=\underset{\rank-k\penalty\ B_{k}}{\min}\|B_{k}-B\|_{1}.

Choose
S\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times n}
to be a dense Cauchy transform matrix with
s=O(r\log r)
. Using Lemma
C.18
, Lemma
D.23
, and combining with Equation (
8
), we have

\displaystyle\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},Z\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}{\min}\|UZSB-B\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{s}O(r\log r)\OPT=O(r^{1.5}\log^{1.5}r)\OPT.

Let
\alpha_{s}=O(r^{1.5}\log^{1.5}r)
.

We define
U^{*},Z^{*}=\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},Z\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\arg\min}\|UZSB-B\|_{1}
. For the fixed
Z^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}
, choose a dense Cauchy transform matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r^{\prime}}
with
r^{\prime}=O(r\log r)
and sketch on the right of
(UZSB-B)
. We obtain the minimization problem,
\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}{\min}\|UZ^{*}SBR-BR\|_{1}
.

Define
\widehat{U}^{j}=B^{j}R((Z^{*}SB)R)^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\forall j\in[n]
. Then
\widehat{U}=BR((Z^{*}SB)R)^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. Due to Claim
B.10
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|B^{j}R((Z^{*}SB)R)^{\dagger}Z^{*}SBR-B^{j}R\|_{1}\leq O(\sqrt{r^{\prime}})\sum_{j=1}^{n}\min_{U^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\|U^{j}Z^{*}SBR-B^{j}R\|_{1},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\|BR((Z^{*}SB)R)^{\dagger}Z^{*}SBR-BR\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{r^{\prime}})\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|UZ^{*}SBR-BR\|_{1},

where
BR
is an
n\times r^{\prime}
matrix and
SB
is an
s\times d
matrix. Both of them can be computed in
(n+d)\poly(r)
time.

Using Lemma
D.8
, Lemma
D.23
, Lemma
C.18
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\|BR((Z^{*}SB)R)^{\dagger}Z^{*}SB-B\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{r^{\prime}})\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|UZ^{*}SB-B\|_{1}

where
\alpha_{r^{\prime}}=\widetilde{O}(r)
.

We define
X^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}
,
Y^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}
,

X^{*},Y^{*}=\underset{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\prime}\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}{\arg\min}\|BRXYSB-B\|_{1}.

Then,

\displaystyle\|BRX^{*}Y^{*}SB-B\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|BR((Z^{*}SB)R)^{\dagger}Z^{*}SB-B\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{r^{\prime}})\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\|UZ^{*}SB-B\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O(\sqrt{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{r^{\prime}})\min_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},Z\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}\|UZSB-B\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{s})\OPT.

It means that
BRX
,
YSB
gives an
O(\alpha_{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{s}\sqrt{r^{\prime}})
-approximation to the original problem.

Thus it suffices to use Lemma
C.20
to solve

\underset{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}{\min}\|BRXYSB-B\|_{1},

by losing an extra
\poly(r)
approximation ratio. Therefore, we finish the proof.
∎

Lemma C.20
.

Suppose we are given
S\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times n}
,
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r^{\prime}}
, and a factorization of a
\rank
-
r
matrix
B=U_{B}V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, where
U_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r},V_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
. Then for any
1\leq k\leq r
, there exists an algorithm which takes
(n+d)\poly(r,r^{\prime},s)
time to output two matrices
X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\prime}\times k},Y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}
such that

\displaystyle\|BRX^{\prime}\cdot Y^{\prime}SB-B\|_{1}\leq\poly(r)\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\prime}\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}\|BRXYSB-B\|_{1}

holds with probability at least
.999
.

Proof.

Choosing dense Cauchy matrices
T_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{1}\times n},T_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{2}\times d}
to sketch on both sides, we get the problem

\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\prime}\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}\|T_{1}BRXYSBT_{2}-T_{1}BT_{2}\|_{1},

(14)

where
t_{1}=\widetilde{O}(r)
and
t_{2}=\widetilde{O}(r)
.

Define
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
to be the optimal solution of

\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\prime}\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}\|T_{1}BRXYSBT_{2}-T_{1}BT_{2}\|_{F}.

Define
\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}
to the be the optimal solution of

\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\prime}\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}\|BRXYSB-B\|_{1}.

By Claim
B.11
,

\displaystyle\|T_{1}BRX^{\prime}Y^{\prime}SBT_{2}-T_{1}BT_{2}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{r^{\prime}\times k},Y\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times s}}\|T_{1}BRXYSBT_{2}-T_{1}BT_{2}\|_{1}.

By Lemma
D.10
, Lemma
C.18
and Lemma
D.23
, we have

\displaystyle\|BRX^{\prime}\cdot Y^{\prime}SB-B\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{O}(r^{2})\|BR\widetilde{X}\cdot\widetilde{Y}SB-B\|_{1}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Contraction and Dilation Bound for
\ell_{1}

This section presents the essential lemmas for
\ell_{1}
-low rank approximation. Section
D.1
gives some basic definitions. Section
D.2
shows some properties implied by contraction and dilation bounds. Section
D.3
presents the no dilation lemma for a dense Cauchy transform. Section
D.4
and
D.5
presents the no contraction lemma for dense Cauchy transforms. Section
D.6
and
D.7
contains the results for sparse Cauchy transforms and Lewis weights.

D.1
Definitions

Definition D.1
.

Given a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, if matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|SM\|_{1}\leq c_{1}\|M\|_{1},

then
S
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
M
.

Definition D.2
.

Given a matrix
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, if matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|Ux\|_{1},

then
S
has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
U
.

Definition D.3
.

Given matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. If matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},\|SUV-SA\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{c_{3}}\|UV-A\|_{1}-c_{4}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1},

then
S
has at most
(c_{3},c_{4})
-contraction on
(U,A)
.

Definition D.4
.

A
(c_{5},c_{6})

\ell_{1}
-subspace embedding for the column space of an
n\times k
matrix
U
is a matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
for which all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{5}}\|Ux\|_{1}\leq\|SUx\|_{1}\leq c_{6}\|Ux\|_{1}.

Definition D.5
.

Given matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}.
If for all
c\geq 1
, and if for any
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SU\widehat{V}-SA\|_{1}\leq c\cdot\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|SUV-SA\|_{1},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}\leq c\cdot c_{7}\cdot\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1},

then
S
provides a
c_{7}
-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(U,A)
.

Definition D.6
.

Given matrices
L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{1}},N\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},k\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle X^{*}=\arg\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|LXN-A\|_{1}.

Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}.
If for all
c\geq 1
, and if for any
\rank-k\ \widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{1}\leq c\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|SLXN-SA\|_{1},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{1}\leq c\cdot c_{8}\cdot\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1},

then
S
provides a
c_{8}
-restricted-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(L,N,A,k)
.

D.2
Properties

Lemma D.7
.

Given matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, let
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. If
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
UV^{*}-A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|S(UV^{*}-A)\|_{1}\leq c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1},

and it has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
U
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|Ux\|_{1},

then
S
has at most
(c_{2},c_{1}+\frac{1}{c_{2}})
-contraction on
(U,A)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},\|SUV-SA\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-A\|_{1}-(c_{1}+\frac{1}{c_{2}})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1},

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be the same as that described in the lemma. Then
\forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}

\displaystyle\|SUV-SA\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\|SUV-SUV^{*}\|_{1}-\|SUV^{*}-SA\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\|SUV-SUV^{*}\|_{1}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|SU(V-V^{*})\|_{1}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|SU(V-V^{*})_{j}\|_{1}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|U(V-V^{*})_{j}\|_{1}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-UV^{*}\|_{1}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-A\|_{1}-\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-A\|_{1}-\left((\frac{1}{c_{2}}+c_{1})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}\right).

The first inequality follows by the triangle inequality. The second inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{1}
dilation on
UV^{*}-A
. The third inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{2}
contraction on
U
. The fourth inequality follows by the triangle inequality.
∎

Lemma D.8
.

Given matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, let
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. If
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
UV^{*}-A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|S(UV^{*}-A)\|_{1}\leq c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1},

and has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
U
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|Ux\|_{1},

then
S
provides a
(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)
-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(U,A)
, i.e.,
for all
c\geq 1
, for any
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SU\widehat{V}-SA\|_{1}\leq c\cdot\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|SUV-SA\|_{1},

it has

\displaystyle\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}\leq c\cdot(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)\cdot\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1},

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*},\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},
and
c
be the same as stated in the lemma.

\displaystyle\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\|SU\widehat{V}-SA\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}c\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|SUV-SA\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}c\|SUV^{*}-SA\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}c_{2}c\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot(1+2c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{1}.

The first inequality follows by Lemma
D.7
. The second inequality follows by the guarantee of
\widehat{V}
. The fourth inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
UV^{*}-A
. The fifth inequality follows since
c\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma D.9
.

Given matrices
L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{1}},N\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},k\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle X^{*}=\arg\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|LXN-A\|_{1}.

If
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
LX^{*}N-A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|S(LX^{*}N-A)\|_{1}\leq c_{1}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1},

and has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
L
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\|SLx\|_{1}\geq\|Lx\|_{1},

then
S
provides a
(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)
-restricted-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(L,N,A,k)
, i.e., for all
c\geq 1
, for any
\rank-k\ \widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{1}\leq c\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|SLXN-SA\|_{1},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{1}\leq c\cdot(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)\cdot\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}.

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{1}},\widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}},X^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}},N\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, and
c\geq 1
be the same as stated in the lemma.

\displaystyle\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\|SL\widehat{X}N-SLX^{*}N\|_{1}-\|SLX^{*}N-SA\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|L(\widehat{X}N-X^{*}N)\|_{1}-c_{1}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{1}-\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}-c_{1}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{1}-(\frac{1}{c_{2}}+c_{1})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}.

The inequality follows by the triangle inequality. The second inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
L
, and it has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
LX^{*}N-A
. The third inequality follows by the triangle inequality.

It follows that

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}c\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|SLXN-SA\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}c\cdot\|SLX^{*}N-SA\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq cc_{1}c_{2}\cdot\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}+(1+c_{1}c_{2})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot(1+2c_{1}c_{2})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}.

The first inequality directly follows from the previous one. The second inequality follows from the guarantee of
\widehat{X}
. The fourth inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{1}
dilation on
LX^{*}N-A
. The fifth inequality follows since
c\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma D.10
.

Given matrices
L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{1}},N\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},k\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle X^{*}=\arg\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|LXN-A\|_{1}.

Let
T_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{1}\times n}
have at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
LX^{*}N-A
, and at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
L
. Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}=\arg\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|T_{1}LXN-T_{1}A\|_{1}.

Let
T_{2}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{t_{2}\times d}
have at most
c^{\prime}_{1}
-dilation on
(T_{1}L\widetilde{X}N-T_{1}A)^{\top}
, and at most
c^{\prime}_{2}
-contraction on
N^{\top}
. Then, for all
c\geq 1
, for any
\rank-k\ \widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|T_{1}(L\widehat{X}N-SA)T_{2}\|_{1}\leq c\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|T_{1}(LXN-A)T_{2}\|_{1},

it has

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{1}\leq c\cdot(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)(2c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}+1)\cdot\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
D.9
for sketch matrix
T_{2}
. Then for any
c\geq 1
, any
\rank-k\ \widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|T_{1}(L\widehat{X}N-A)T_{2}\|_{1}\leq c\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|T_{1}(LXN-A)T_{2}\|_{1},

has

\displaystyle\|T_{1}(L\widehat{X}N-A)\|_{1}\leq c\cdot(2c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}+1)\cdot\|T_{1}(L\widetilde{X}N-A)\|_{1}.

Apply Lemma
D.9
for sketch matrix
T_{1}
. Then for any
c\geq 1
, any
\rank-k\ \widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|T_{1}(L\widehat{X}N-A)\|_{1}\leq c(2c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}+1)\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|T_{1}(L\widetilde{X}N-A)\|_{1},

has

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{1}\leq c\cdot(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)(2c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}+1)\cdot\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}.

∎

Lemma D.11
.

Given matrices
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times t}
,
d\geq t=\rank(U),n\geq d\geq r=\rank(M)
, if sketching matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is drawn from any of the following probability distributions of matrices, with
.99
probability
S
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
M
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|SM\|_{1}\leq c_{1}\|M\|_{1},

and
S
has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
U
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{t},\ \|SUx\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|Ux\|_{1},

where
c_{1},\ c_{2}
are parameters depend on the distribution over
S
.

(I)

S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a dense Cauchy matrix: a matrix with i.i.d. entries from the standard Cauchy distribution. If
m=O(t\log t)
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(\log d)
. If
m=O((t+r)\log(t+r))
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(\min(\log d,r\log r))
.

(II)

S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a sparse Cauchy matrix:
S=TD
, where
T\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has each column i.i.d. from the uniform distribution on standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a diagonal matrix with i.i.d. diagonal entries following a standard Cauchy distribution. If
m=O(t^{5}\log^{5}t)
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(t^{2}\log^{2}t\log d)
. If
m=O((t+r)^{5}\log^{5}(t+r))
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(\min(t^{2}\log^{2}t\log d,r^{3}\log^{3}r))
.

(III)

S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a sampling and rescaling matrix (notation
S\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
denotes a diagonal sampling and rescaling matrix with
m
non-zero entries): If
S
samples and reweights
m=O(t\log t)
rows of
U
, selecting each with probability proportional to the
i^{\text{th}}
row’s
\ell_{1}
Lewis weight and reweighting by the inverse probability, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(1)
.

(IV)

S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
is a dense Cauchy matrix with limited independence:
S
is a matrix with each entry drawn from a standard Cauchy distribution. Entries from different rows of
S
are fully independent, and entries from the same row of
S
are
W
-wise independent. If
m=O(t\log t)
, and
W=\widetilde{O}(d)
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(t\log d)
. If
m=O(t\log t)
, and
W=\widetilde{O}(td)
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(\log d)
.

In the above, if we replace
S
with
\sigma\cdot S
where
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
is any scalar, then the relation between
m
and
c_{1}c_{2}
can be preserved.

For
\rm{(I)}
, if
m=O(t\log t)
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(\log d)
is implied by Lemma
D.23
and Lemma
D.13
. If
m=O((t+r)\log(t+r))
,
c_{1}c_{2}=O(r\log r)
is implied by
[
SW11
]
.

For
\rm{(II)}
, if
m=O(t^{5}\log^{5}t)
, then
c_{1}c_{2}=O(t^{2}\log^{2}t\log d)
is implied by Corollary
D.27
and Lemma
D.25
. If
m=O((t+r)^{5}\log^{5}(t+r))
,
c_{1}c_{2}=O(r^{3}\log^{3}r)
is implied by
[
MM13
]
.

For
\rm{(III)}
, it is implied by
[
CP15
]
and Lemma
D.29
.

For
\rm{(IV)}
, it is implied by Lemma
I.4
, Corollary
I.5
.

D.3
Cauchy embeddings, no dilation

Lemma D.12
.

Define
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be the optimal solution of
\underset{{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}}{\min}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. Choose a Cauchy matrix
S
with
m
rows and rescaled by
\Theta(1/m)
. We have that

\displaystyle\|SU^{*}V^{*}-SA\|_{1}\leq O(\log d)\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability at least
99/100
.

Proof.

The proof technique has been used in
[
Ind06
]
and
[
CDMI+13
]
. Fix the optimal
U^{*}
and
V^{*}
, then

\displaystyle\|SU^{*}V^{*}-SA\|_{1}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|S(U^{*}V_{i}^{*}-A_{i})\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m}S_{j,l}\cdot(U^{*}V_{i}^{*}-A_{i})_{l}|

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }S_{j,l}\sim C(0,1)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{m}|c_{i,j}|

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }c_{i,j}\sim C(0,\|U^{*}V_{i}^{*}-A_{i}\|_{1})

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|U^{*}V_{i}^{*}-A_{i}\|_{1}\cdot w_{i+d(j-1)}.

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }w_{i+d(j-1)}\sim|C(0,1)|

(15)

where the last step follows since each
w_{i}
can be thought of as a clipped half-Cauchy random variable. Define
d^{\prime}=md
.
Define event
\xi_{i}
to be the situation in which
w_{i}<D
(we will decide upon
D
later), and define event
\xi=\xi_{1}\cap\xi_{2}\cap\cdots\cap\xi_{d^{\prime}}
. Then it is clear that
\xi\cap\xi_{i}=\xi,\forall i\in[d^{\prime}]
.

Using the probability density function (pdf) of a
Cauchy and because
\tan^{-1}x\leq x
, we can lower bound
\Pr[\text{event\penalty\ }\xi_{i}\penalty\ \text{holds}]
in the following sense,

\displaystyle\Pr[\xi_{i}]=\frac{2}{\pi}\tan^{-1}(D)=1-\frac{2}{\pi}\tan^{-1}(1/D)\geq 1-\frac{2}{\pi D}.

By a union bound over all
i\in[d^{\prime}]
, we can lower bound
\Pr[\text{event\penalty\ }\xi\penalty\ \text{holds}]
,

\displaystyle\Pr[\xi]\geq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{d^{\prime}}\Pr[\overline{\xi}_{i}]\geq 1-\frac{2d^{\prime}}{\pi D}.

(16)

By Bayes rule and
\xi=\xi\cap\xi_{i}
,
\Pr[\xi|\xi_{i}]\Pr[\xi_{i}]=\Pr[\xi\cap\xi_{i}]=\Pr[\xi]
, which implies that
\Pr[\xi|\xi_{i}]=\Pr[\xi]/\Pr[\xi_{i}]
. First, we can lower bound
\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}]
,

\displaystyle\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}]=\penalty\

\displaystyle\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}\cap\xi]\Pr[\xi|\xi_{i}]+\E[w_{i}|\xi\cap\overline{\xi}]\Pr[\overline{\xi}|\xi_{i}]

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}\cap\xi]\Pr[\xi|\xi_{i}]

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }w_{i}\geq 0\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\Pr[]\geq 0

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\E[w_{i}|\xi]\Pr[\xi|\xi_{i}]

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\xi=\xi\cap\xi_{i}.

The above equation implies that

\displaystyle\E[w_{i}|\xi]\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}]}{\Pr[\xi|\xi_{i}]}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}]\Pr[\xi_{i}]}{\Pr[\xi\cap\xi_{i}]}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ Bayes\penalty\ rule\penalty\ }\Pr[\xi|\xi_{i}]\Pr[\xi_{i}]=\Pr[\xi\cap\xi_{i}]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}]\Pr[\xi_{i}]}{\Pr[\xi]}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\xi=\xi\cap\xi_{i}.

Using the pdf of a Cauchy,
\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}]=\frac{1}{\pi}\log(1+D^{2})/\Pr[\xi_{i}]
and plugging it into the lower bound of
\E[w_{i}|\xi]
,

\displaystyle\E[w_{i}|\xi]\leq\frac{\E[w_{i}|\xi_{i}]\Pr[\xi_{i}]}{\Pr[\xi]}=\frac{\frac{1}{\pi}\log(1+D^{2})}{\Pr[\xi]}\leq\frac{\frac{1}{\pi}\log(1+D^{2})}{1-\frac{2d}{\pi D}}\lesssim\log(D),

where the third step follows since
\Pr[\xi]\geq 1-\frac{2d^{\prime}}{\pi D}
and the last step follows by choosing
D=\Theta(d^{\prime})
.

We can conclude

\displaystyle\E[\|SU^{*}V^{*}-SA\|_{1}|\xi]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|U^{*}V_{i}^{*}-A_{i}\|_{1}\cdot\E[w_{i+d(j-1)}|\xi]\lesssim(\log d^{\prime})\cdot\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}.

(17)

For simplicity, define
X=\|SU^{*}V^{*}-SA\|_{1}
and
\gamma=\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}
.
By Markov’s inequality and because
\Pr[X\geq\gamma t|\overline{\xi}]\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[X\geq\gamma t]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\Pr[X\geq\gamma t|\xi]\Pr[\xi]+\Pr[X\geq\gamma t|\overline{\xi}]\Pr[\overline{\xi}]

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\Pr[X\geq\gamma t|\xi]+\Pr[\overline{\xi}]

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\E[X|\xi]}{\gamma t}+\Pr[\overline{\xi}]

by Markov’s inequality

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\E[X|\xi]}{\gamma t}+\frac{2d^{\prime}}{\pi D}

by Equation (
16
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\log d^{\prime}}{t}+\frac{2d^{\prime}}{\pi D}

by Equation (
17
)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle.01,

where choosing
t=\Theta(\log d^{\prime})
and
D=\Theta(d^{\prime})
. Since
k\leq d
and
m=\poly(k)
, we have
t=\Theta(\log d)
, which completes the proof.

∎

Lemma D.13
.

Given any matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, if matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has each entry drawn from an i.i.d. standard Cauchy distribution and is rescaled by
\Theta(1/m)
, then

\displaystyle\|SM\|_{1}\leq O(\log d)\|M\|_{1}

holds with probability at least
99/100
.

Proof.

Just replace the matrix
U^{*}V^{*}-A
in the proof of Lemma
D.12
with
M
. Then we can get the result directly.
∎

D.4
Cauchy embeddings, no contraction

We prove that if we choose a Cauchy matrix
S
, then for a fixed optimal solution
U^{*}
of
\min_{U,V}\|UV-A\|_{1}
, and for all
V
, we have that with high probability
\|SU^{*}V-SA\|_{1}
is lower bounded by
\|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}
up to some constant.

Lemma D.14
.

Define
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be the optimal solution of
\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|UV-A\|_{1}
, where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
m=O(k\log k),S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a random matrix with each entry an i.i.d. standard Cauchy random variable, scaled by
\Theta(1/m)
. Then with probability at least
0.95
,

\displaystyle\forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},\|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}\lesssim\|SU^{*}V-SA\|_{1}+O(\log(d))\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}.

Proof.

This follows by Lemmas
D.12
,
D.23
,
D.7
.

∎

D.5
Cauchy embeddings,
k
-dimensional subspace

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
D.23
.

Before getting into the details, we first give the formal definition of an
(\alpha,\beta)

\ell_{1}
well-conditioned basis and
\epsilon
-net.

Definition D.15

(
\ell_{1}
Well-conditioned basis)
.

[
DDH+09
]

A basis
U
for the range of
A
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-conditioned if
\|U\|_{1}\leq\alpha
and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\beta\|Ux\|_{1}
. We will say
U
is well-conditioned if
\alpha
and
\beta
are low-degree polynomials in
k
, independent of
n
.

Note that a well-conditioned basis implies the following result.

Fact D.16
.

There exist
\alpha,\beta\geq 1
such that,

\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\frac{1}{k\beta}\|x\|_{1}\leq\|Ux\|_{1}\leq\alpha\|x\|_{1}.

Proof.

The lower bound can be proved in the following sense,

\|Ux\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\|x\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\frac{1}{k}\|x\|_{1},

where the first step follows by the properties of a well-conditioned basis, and the second step follows since
k\|x\|_{\infty}\geq\|x\|_{1}
.
Then we can show an upper bound,

\|Ux\|_{1}\leq\|U\|_{1}\cdot\|x\|_{1}\leq\alpha\|x\|_{1},

where the first step follows by
\|Ux\|_{1}\leq\|U\|_{1}\|x\|_{1}
, and the second step follows using

\|U\|_{1}\leq\alpha
.
∎

Definition D.17

(
\epsilon
-net)
.

Define
{\cal N}
to an
\epsilon
-net where, for all the
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
that
\|x\|_{1}=1
, for any vectors two
x,x^{\prime}\in{\cal N}
,
\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{1}\geq\epsilon
, for any vector
y\notin{\cal N}
, there exists an
x\in{\cal N}
such that
\|x-y\|_{1}\leq\epsilon
. Then the size of
{\cal N}
is
(\frac{1}{\epsilon})^{O(k)}=2^{O(k\log(1/\epsilon))}
.

Lemma D.18

(Lemma 6 in
[
SW11
]
)
.

There is a constant
c_{0}>0
such that for any
t\geq 1
and any constant
c>c_{0}
, if
S
is a
t\times n
matrix whose entries are i.i.d. standard Cauchy random variables scaled by
c/t
, then, for any fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\Pr[\|Sy\|_{1}<\|y\|_{1}]\leq 1/2^{t}

Lemma D.19
.

Suppose we are given a well-conditioned basis
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. If
S
is a
t\times n
matrix whose entries are i.i.d. standard Cauchy random variables scaled by
\Theta(1/t)
, then with probability
1-2^{-\Omega(t)}
, for all vectors
x\in{\mathcal{N}}
we have that
\|SUx\|_{1}\geq\|Ux\|_{1}
.

Proof.

First, using Lemma
D.18
, we have for any fixed vector
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\Pr[\|Sy\|<\|y\|_{1}]\leq 1/2^{t}
. Second, we can rewrite
y=Ux
. Then for any fixed
x\in\mathcal{N}
,
\Pr[\|SUx\|<\|Ux\|_{1}]\leq 1/2^{t}
.
Third, choosing
t\gtrsim k\log(1/\epsilon)
and taking a union bound over all the vectors in the
\epsilon
-net
\mathcal{N}
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.20

(Lemma 7 in
[
SW11
]
)
.

Let
S
be a
t\times n
matrix whose entries are i.i.d standard Cauchy random variables, scaled by
c/t
for a constant
c
, and
t\geq 1
. Then there is a constant
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}(c)>0
such that for any fixed set of
\{y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{k}\}
of
d
vectors in
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},Ux=y\}
,

\Pr[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|Sy_{i}\|_{1}\geq c^{\prime}\log(tk)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|y_{i}\|_{1}]\leq\frac{1}{1000}.

Using Lemma
D.20
and the definition of an
(\alpha,\beta)
well-conditioned basis, we can show the following corollary.

Corollary D.21
.

Suppose we are given an
(\alpha,\beta)

\ell_{1}
well-conditioned basis
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. Choose
S
to be an i.i.d. Cauchy matrix with
t=O(k\log k)
rows, and rescale each entry by
\Theta(1/t)
. Then with probability
99/100
, for all vectors
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\|SUx\|_{1}\leq O(\alpha\beta\log(k))\cdot\|Ux\|_{1},

Proof.

Define event
E
to be the situation when

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|SU_{i}\|_{1}<c^{\prime}\log(tk)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|U_{i}\|_{1}

holds, and
U
is a well-conditioned basis. Using Lemma
D.20
, we can show that event
E
holds with probability
999/1000
. We condition on Event
E
holding. Then for any
y=Ux
for an
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\|Sy\|_{1}\penalty\

\displaystyle=\|SUx\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|SU_{j}x_{j}\|_{1}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}|x_{j}|\cdot\|SU_{j}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|x\|_{\infty}c^{\prime}\log(tk)\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|U_{j}\|_{1}

by Lemma
D.20

\displaystyle=\|x\|_{\infty}c^{\prime}\log(tk)\alpha

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\|U\|_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\|U_{j}\|_{1}\leq\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|Ux\|_{1}c^{\prime}\log(tk)\alpha

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\|x\|_{\infty}\leq\beta\|Ux\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|y\|_{1}c^{\prime}\log(tk)\alpha

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }Ux=y.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.22
.

Given an
(\alpha,\beta)

\ell_{1}
well-conditioned basis, condition on the following two events,

1. For all
x\in{\mathcal{N}}
,
\|SUx\|_{1}\geq\|Ux\|_{1}
. (Lemma
D.18
)

2. For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\|SUx\|_{1}\leq O(\alpha\beta\log k)\|Ux\|_{1}
. (Corollary
D.21
)

Then, for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\|SUw\|_{1}\gtrsim\|Uw\|_{1}
.

Proof.

For any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we can write it as
w=\ell\cdot z
where
\ell
is some scalar and
z
has
\|z\|_{1}=1
. Define
y=\underset{y^{\prime}\in{\cal N}}{\arg\min}\|y^{\prime}-z\|_{1}
.

We first show that if
U
is an
(\alpha,\beta)
well-conditioned basis for
\ell_{1}
, then
\|U(y-z)\|_{1}\leq\alpha\beta k\epsilon\|Uy\|_{1}
,

\displaystyle\|U(y-z)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\|y-z\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\|U(y-z)\|_{1}\leq\alpha\|y-z\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\epsilon

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\|y-z\|_{1}\leq\epsilon

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\epsilon\|y\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\|y\|_{1}=1

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\alpha\epsilon\|Uy\|_{1}\beta k

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\|Uy\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{\beta k}\|y\|_{1}.

Because
\epsilon<1/(\alpha\beta k^{c+1})
, we have

\|U(y-z)\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{k^{c}}\|Uy\|_{1}.

(18)

Using the triangle inequality, we can lower bound
\|Uy\|_{1}
by
\|Uz\|_{1}
up to some constant,

\|Uy\|_{1}\geq\|Uz\|_{1}-\|U(y-z)\|_{1}\geq\|Uz\|_{1}-\frac{1}{k^{c}}\|Uy\|_{1},

(19)

which implies

\|Uy\|_{1}\geq.99\|Uz\|_{1}.

(20)

Thus,

\displaystyle\|SUz\|_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|SUy\|_{1}-\|SU(z-y)\|_{1}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|Uy\|_{1}-\|SU(z-y)\|_{1}

by Lemma
D.18

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|Uy\|_{1}-\alpha\beta\log(k)\cdot\|U(z-y)\|_{1}

by Corollary
D.21

\displaystyle\geq\penalty\

\displaystyle\|Uy\|_{1}-\alpha\beta\log(k)\cdot\frac{1}{k^{c}}\|Uy\|_{1}

by Equation (
18
)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\penalty\

\displaystyle\|Uy\|_{1}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }k^{c}\gtrsim\alpha\beta\log(k)

\displaystyle\gtrsim\penalty\

\displaystyle\|Uz\|_{1},

by Equation (
20
)

by rescaling
z
to
w
, we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma D.23
.

Given matrix
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, let
t=O(k\log k)
, and let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{t\times n}
be a random matrix with entries drawn i.i.d. from a standard Cauchy distribution, where each entry is rescaled by
\Theta(1/t)
. With probability
.99
,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{1}\gtrsim\|Ux\|_{1}.

Proof.

We can compute a well-conditioned basis for
U
, and denote it
U^{\prime}
. Then,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, there exists
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
Ux=U^{\prime}y
. Due to Lemma
D.22
, with probability
.99
, we have

\displaystyle\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SU^{\prime}y\|_{1}\gtrsim\|U^{\prime}y\|_{1}.

∎

D.6
Sparse Cauchy transform

This section presents the proof of two lemmas related to the sparse Cauchy transform. We first prove the no dilation result in Lemma
D.24
. Then we show how to get the no contraction result in Lemma
D.26
.

Lemma D.24
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
U^{*},V^{*}
be the optimal solutions of
\min_{U,V}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. Let
\Pi=\sigma\cdot SC\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, where
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has each column vector chosen independently and uniformly from the
m
standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, where
C
is a diagonal matrix with diagonals chosen independently from the standard Cauchy distribution, and
\sigma
is a scalar. Then

\displaystyle\|\Pi U^{*}V^{*}-\Pi A\|_{1}\lesssim\sigma\cdot\log(md)\cdot\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}

holds with probability at least
.999
.

Proof.

We define
\Pi=\sigma\cdot SC\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, as in the statement
of the lemma. Then,

\displaystyle\penalty\ \|\Pi(U^{*}V^{*}-A)\|_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\|SD(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})\|_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\biggl\|\begin{bmatrix}S_{11}&S_{12}&\cdots&S_{1n}\\
S_{21}&S_{22}&\cdots&S_{2n}\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
S_{m1}&S_{m2}&\cdots&S_{mn}\\
\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}&0&0&0\\
0&c_{2}&0&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0&c_{n}\\
\end{bmatrix}\cdot(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})\biggr\|_{1}\penalty\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\biggl\|\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}S_{11}&c_{2}S_{12}&\cdots&c_{n}S_{1n}\\
c_{1}S_{21}&c_{2}S_{22}&\cdots&c_{n}S_{2n}\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
c_{1}S_{m1}&c_{2}S_{m2}&\cdots&c_{n}S_{mn}\\
\end{bmatrix}\cdot(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})\biggr\|_{1}\penalty\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\biggl\|\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{l}S_{1l}\cdot(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l},\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{l}S_{2l}\cdot(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l},\cdots,\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{l}S_{ml}\cdot(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}\biggr\|_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|\sum_{l=1}^{n}c_{l}S_{jl}\cdot(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|\widetilde{w}_{ij}\cdot\sum_{l=1}^{n}|S_{jl}(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}||\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }\widetilde{w}_{ij}\sim C(0,1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{n}|S_{jl}(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}|\cdot|\widetilde{w}_{ij}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{n}|S_{jl}(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}|\cdot w_{i+(j-1)d}\text{\penalty\ where\penalty\ }w_{i+(j-1)d}\sim|C(0,1)|,

where the last step follows since each
w_{i}
can be thought of as a clipped half-Cauchy random variable.
Define
d^{\prime}=md
. Define event
\xi_{i}
to be the situation when
w_{i}<D
(we will decide upon
D
later).
Define event
\xi=\xi_{1}\cap\xi_{2}\cap\cdots\cap\xi_{d^{\prime}}
.
By choosing
D=\Theta(d^{\prime})
, we can conclude that,

\displaystyle\E[\|\Pi U^{*}V^{*}-\Pi A\|_{1}|\xi]

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{n}|S_{jl}(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}|\cdot\E[w_{i+(j-1)d}|\xi]

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{l=1}^{n}|S_{jl}(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}|\cdot\log(d^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{l=1}^{n}|(U^{*}V^{*}_{i}-A_{i})_{l}|_{1}\cdot\log(d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ by\penalty\ }\sum_{j=1}^{m}|S_{jl}|=1,\forall l\in[n]

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \log(d^{\prime})\cdot\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}.

Thus, we can show that

\displaystyle\Pr[\|\Pi U^{*}V^{*}-\Pi A\|_{1}\lesssim\log(d^{\prime})\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}]\geq 0.999.

∎

Lemma D.25
.

Given any matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
\Pi=\sigma\cdot SC\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, where
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has each column vector chosen independently and uniformly from the
m
standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, where
C
is a diagonal matrix with diagonals chosen independently from the standard Cauchy distribution, and
\sigma
is a scalar. Then

\displaystyle\|\Pi M\|_{1}\lesssim\sigma\cdot\log(md)\cdot\|M\|_{1}

holds with probability at least
.999
.

Proof.

Just replace the matrix
U^{*}V^{*}-A
in the proof of Lemma
D.24
with
M
. Then we can get the result directly.
∎

We already provided the proof of Lemma
D.24
. It remains to prove Lemma
D.26
.

Lemma D.26
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
U^{*},V^{*}
be the optimal solution of
\min_{U,V}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. Let
\Pi=\sigma\cdot SC\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, where
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has each column vector chosen independently and uniformly from the
m
standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and where
C
is a diagonal matrix with diagonals chosen independently from the standard Cauchy distribution. Then with probability at least
.999
, for all
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,

\displaystyle\|\Pi U^{*}V-\Pi A\|_{1}\geq\|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}-O(\sigma\cdot\log(md))\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}.

Notice that
m=O(k^{5}\log^{5}k)
and
\sigma=O(k^{2}\log^{2}k)
according to Theorem 2 in
[
MM13
]
.

We start by using Theorem 2 in
[
MM13
]
to generate the following Corollary.

Corollary D.27
.

Given
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
with full column rank, let
\Pi=\sigma\cdot SC\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
where
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has each column chosen independently and uniformly from the
m
standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and where
C\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a diagonal matrix with diagonals chosen independently from the standard Cauchy distribution. Then with probability
.999
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Pi Ux\|_{1}\geq\|Ux\|_{1}.

Notice that
m=O(k^{5}\log^{5}k)
and
\sigma=O(k^{2}\log^{2}k)
according to Theorem 2 in
[
MM13
]
.

We give the proof of Lemma
D.26
.

Proof.

Follows by Corollary
D.27
, Lemma
D.24
, Lemma
D.7
.
∎

D.7
\ell_{1}
-Lewis weights

In this section we show how to use Lewis weights to get a better dilation bound. The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
D.32
and Lemma
D.31
. Notice that our algorithms in Section
C
use Lewis weights in several different ways. The first way is using Lewis weights to show an existence result, which means we only need an existential result for Lewis weights. The second way is only guessing the nonzero locations on the diagonal of a sampling and rescaling matrix according to the Lewis weights. The third way is guessing the values on the diagonal of a sampling and rescaling matrix according to the Lewis weights. The fourth way is computing the Lewis weights for a known low dimensional matrix(
n\times\poly(k)
or
\poly(k)\times d
). We usually do not need to optimize the running time of computing Lewis weights for a low-rank matrix to have input-sparsity time.

Claim D.28
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
B=U^{*}V^{*}-A
. For any distribution
p=(p_{1},p_{2},\dotsc,p_{n})
define random variable
X
such that
X=\|B_{i}\|_{1}/p_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
. Then take any
m
independent samples
X^{1},X^{2},\dotsc,X^{m}
, let
Y=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}X^{j}
. We have

\Pr[Y\leq 1000\|B\|_{1}]\geq.999

Proof.

We can compute the expectation of
X^{j}
, for any
j\in[m]

\E[X^{j}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\|B_{i}\|_{1}}{p_{i}}\cdot p_{i}=\|B\|_{1}.

Then,
\E[Y]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\E[X^{j}]=\|B\|_{1}
. Using Markov’s inequality, we have

\Pr[Y\geq 1000\|B\|_{1}]\leq.001

∎

Lemma D.29
.

Given matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be any sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix. Then with probability at least
.999
,

\displaystyle\|SM\|_{1}\lesssim\|M\|_{1}.

Proof.

Just replace the matrix
B
in the proof of Claim
D.28
with
M
. Then we can get the result directly.
∎

Using Theorem 1.1 of
[
CP15
]
, we have the following result,

Claim D.30
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, for any fixed
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, choose
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
to be the sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix with
m=O(k\log k)
nonzeros according to the Lewis weights of
U^{*}
. Then with probability
.999
, for all
V
,

\|U^{*}V^{*}-U^{*}V\|_{1}\leq\|DU^{*}V^{*}-DU^{*}V\|_{1}\lesssim\|U^{*}V^{*}-U^{*}V\|_{1}.

Lemma D.31
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, define
V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be the optimal solution of

\underset{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}
. Choose a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
m=O(k\log k)
non-zero entries according to the Lewis weights of
U^{*}
. Then with probability at least
.99
, we have: for all
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,

\|DU^{*}V-DA\|_{1}\lesssim\|U^{*}V^{*}-U^{*}V\|_{1}+O(1)\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}\lesssim\|U^{*}V-A\|_{1},

holds with probability at least
.99
.

Proof.

Using the above two claims, we have with probability at least
.99
, for all
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,

\displaystyle\|DU^{*}V-DA\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \|DU^{*}V-DU^{*}V^{*}\|_{1}+\|DU^{*}V^{*}-DA\|_{1}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\ \|DU^{*}V-DU^{*}V^{*}\|_{1}+O(1)\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}

by Claim
D.28

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\ \|U^{*}V-U^{*}V^{*}\|_{1}+O(1)\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}

by Claim
D.30

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}+\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}+O(1)\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1}

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\ \|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}.

∎

Lemma D.32
.

Given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, define
U^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
to be the optimal solution of

\underset{U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}{\min}\|UV-A\|_{1}
. Choose a sampling and rescaling diagonal matrix
D\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
m=O(k\log k)
non-zero entries according to the Lewis weights of
U^{*}
. For all
V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
we have

\displaystyle\|U^{*}V-A\|_{1}\lesssim\|DU^{*}V-DA\|_{1}+O(1)\|U^{*}V^{*}-A\|_{1},

holds with probability at least
.99
.

Proof.

Follows by Claim
D.28
, Lemma
D.31
, Lemma
D.7
.

∎

Appendix E
\ell_{p}
-Low Rank Approximation

This section presents some fundamental lemmas for
\ell_{p}
-low rank approximation problems. Using these lemmas, all the algorithms described for
\ell_{1}
-low rank approximation problems can be extended to
\ell_{p}
-low rank approximation directly. We only state the important Lemmas in this section, due to most of the proofs in this section being identical to the proofs in Section
D
.

E.1
Definitions

This section is just a generalization of Section
D.1
to the
\ell_{p}
setting when
1<p<2
.

Definition E.1
.

Given a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, if matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|SM\|_{p}^{p}\leq c_{1}\|M\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
M
.

Definition E.2
.

Given a matrix
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, if matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{p}^{p}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|Ux\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
U
.

Definition E.3
.

Given matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, denote
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}
. If matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},\|SUV-SA\|_{p}^{p}\geq\frac{1}{c_{3}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}-c_{4}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
has at most
(c_{3},c_{4})
-contraction on
(U,A)
.

Definition E.4
.

A
(c_{5},c_{6})

\ell_{p}
-subspace embedding for the column space of an
n\times k
matrix
U
is a matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
for which all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{5}}\|Ux\|_{p}^{p}\leq\|SUx\|_{p}^{p}\leq c_{6}\|Ux\|_{p}^{p}.

Definition E.5
.

Given matrices
U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, denote
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}
. Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}.
If for all
c\geq 1
, and if for any
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SU\widehat{V}-SA\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|SUV-SA\|_{p}^{p},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot c_{7}\cdot\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
provides a
c_{7}
-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(U,A)
.

Definition E.6
.

Given matrices
L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{1}},N\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},k\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle X^{*}=\arg\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|LXN-A\|_{p}^{p}.

Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}.
If for all
c\geq 1
, and if for any
\rank-k\ \widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|SLXN-SA\|_{p}^{p},

it holds that

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot c_{8}\cdot\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
provides a
c_{8}
-restricted-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(L,N,A,k)
.

E.2
Properties

Lemma E.7
.

Given matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, let
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}
. If
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
UV^{*}-A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|S(UV^{*}-A)\|_{p}^{p}\leq c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p},

and it has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
U
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{p}^{p}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|Ux\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
has at most
(2^{2p-2}c_{2},c_{1}+2^{1-p}\frac{1}{c_{2}})
-contraction on
(U,A)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},\|SUV-SA\|_{p}^{p}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}-(c_{1}+\frac{1}{c_{2}})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p},

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, and
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be the same as that described in the lemma. Then
\forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}

\displaystyle\|SUV-SA\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{1-p}\|SUV-SUV^{*}\|_{p}^{p}-\|SUV^{*}-SA\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{1-p}\|SUV-SUV^{*}\|_{p}^{p}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{1-p}\|SU(V-V^{*})\|_{p}^{p}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{1-p}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|SU(V-V^{*})_{j}\|_{p}^{p}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{1-p}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|U(V-V^{*})_{j}\|_{p}^{p}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{1-p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-UV^{*}\|_{p}^{p}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{2-2p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}-2^{1-p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}-c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{2-2p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}-\left((2^{1-p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}+c_{1})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}\right).

The first inequality follows by Fact
E.14
. The second inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{1}
dilation on
UV^{*}-A
. The third inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{2}
contraction on
U
. The fourth inequality follows by Fact
E.14
.
∎

Lemma E.8
.

Given matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, let
V^{*}=\arg\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|UV-A\|_{p}^{p}
. If
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
UV^{*}-A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|S(UV^{*}-A)\|_{p}^{p}\leq c_{1}\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p},

and has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
U
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\|SUx\|_{p}^{p}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|Ux\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
provides a
2^{p-1}(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)
-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(U,A)
, i.e.,
for all
c\geq 1
, for any
\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SU\widehat{V}-SA\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|SUV-SA\|_{p}^{p},

it has

\displaystyle\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot 2^{p-1}(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)\cdot\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p},

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},U\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k},V^{*},\widehat{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},
and
c
be the same as stated in the lemma.

\displaystyle\|U\widehat{V}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}c_{2}\|SU\widehat{V}-SA\|_{p}^{p}+(2^{p-1}+2^{2p-2}c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}c_{2}c\min_{V\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}}\|SUV-SA\|_{p}^{p}+(2^{p-1}+2^{2p-2}c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}c_{2}c\|SUV^{*}-SA\|_{p}^{p}+(2^{p-1}+2^{2p-2}c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}c_{1}c_{2}c\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}+(2^{p-1}+2^{2p-2}c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot 2^{p-1}(1+2c_{1}c_{2})\|UV^{*}-A\|_{p}^{p}.

The first inequality follows by Lemma
E.7
. The second inequality follows by the guarantee of
\widehat{V}
. The fourth inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
UV^{*}-A
. The fifth inequality follows since
c\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma E.9
.

Given matrices
L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{1}},N\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},k\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle X^{*}=\arg\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|LXN-A\|_{p}^{p}.

If
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
LX^{*}N-A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|S(LX^{*}N-A)\|_{p}^{p}\leq c_{1}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{p}^{p},

and has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
L
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\|SLx\|_{p}^{p}\geq\|Lx\|_{p}^{p},

then
S
provides a
2^{p-1}(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)
-restricted-multiple-regression-cost preserving sketch of
(L,N,A,k)
, i.e., for all
c\geq 1
, for any
\rank-k\ \widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot\min_{\rank-k\ X}\|SLXN-SA\|_{p}^{p},

it has

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{p}^{p}\leq c\cdot 2^{p-1}(2c_{1}c_{2}+1)\cdot\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{p}^{p}.

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},L\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{1}},\widehat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}},X^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}},N\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{2}\times d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
c\geq 1
be the same as stated in the lemma.

\displaystyle\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{1-p}\|SL\widehat{X}N-SLX^{*}N\|_{p}^{p}-\|SLX^{*}N-SA\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{1-p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|L(\widehat{X}N-X^{*}N)\|_{p}^{p}-c_{1}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{2-2p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{p}^{p}-2^{1-p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{1}-c_{1}\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{2-2p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{p}^{p}-(2^{1-p}\frac{1}{c_{2}}+c_{1})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{p}^{p}.

The inequality follows from the Fact
E.14
. The second inequality follows since
S
has at most
c_{2}
-contraction on
L
, and it has at most
c_{1}
-dilation on
LX^{*}N-A
. The third inequality follows by Fact
E.14
.

It follows that

\displaystyle\|L\widehat{X}N-A\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}c_{2}\|SL\widehat{X}N-SA\|_{p}^{p}+(2^{p-1}+2^{2p-2}c_{1}c_{2})\|LX^{*}N-A\|_{p}^{p}
